Layering specifically removes that connected line, so it would not match this description. Elevation is necessarily involved whenever graduation or layering is built, and razor work is a separate tool choice, not identifiable from perimeter shape alone.
  43. The correct answer is B (These findings suggest the service was processed appropriately without overprocessing occurring.): Normal elasticity, no breakage, and a smooth, healthy-looking result a week after a relaxer service together suggest the hair was processed appropriately without overprocessing, since these are the same measures used to detect damage when it is present. This combination of findings is meaningful rather than inconclusive, since it reflects the absence of the recognized signs of overprocessing. A healthy result does not indicate underprocessing, since underprocessed hair would show inadequate straightening rather than good elasticity and shine. Shine alone would not be sufficient on its own without the other findings also being normal.
  44. The correct answer is A (Use the highest temperature range a quality thermal tool offers.): Coarse, resistant, or virgin hair that has not been chemically treated can generally tolerate the highest range on a quality thermal tool. This is because it has more structural reserve than fine or processed hair. Even so, the stylist should start at the lowest realistic setting and raise it only if needed, so the highest range is the upper limit rather than the starting point.
  45. The correct answer is C (Chemical resistant gloves are not needed for a dry cut with no chemical exposure.): PPE is selected for the specific task at hand rather than worn uniformly for every service, and a dry haircut with no chemical exposure does not call for chemical resistant gloves. Requiring the same gloves, eye protection, or a protective apron for every service regardless of what it involves misunderstands how PPE selection actually works. A stylist mixing lightener needs different protection than one performing a dry cut. Matching PPE to the actual hazard present is the standard the exam expects a candidate to apply.
  46. The cap limits how much hair can be pulled at each hole, which is what gives a fine, uniform highlight. It suits smooth, long enough hair, and does not produce bold placement or multi-formula work.
  47. Introducing high elevation or graduation would change the fundamental shape rather than simply trim it. Respecting the established technique keeps the maintenance trim consistent with what the client already has and wants to keep.
  48. The correct answer is B (The disulfide bonds must be fully broken so the cortex becomes soft and pliable.): For a perm to reshape the hair, the reducing agent must break enough disulfide bonds to make the cortex soft and workable around the rod. This is the chemical basis for the whole service and is what processing time is monitored against. Stripping the cuticle, heat drying, and rinsing before timing are not part of how a perm actually works, and none of them substitute for the bond-breaking action of the reducing solution.
  49. The correct answer is C (A firm backcombed foundation, secure pinning, and a strong hold finishing product.): Durability techniques matter more than in routine styling, so a firm backcombed foundation, secure and evenly placed pinning, and a strong-hold finishing product extend how long a special occasion style holds its shape. Humidity resistant styling products and a supply of extra pins are reasonable contingencies for a wedding day. A flexible light hold or loose pinning does not give the same staying power.
  50. The correct answer is D (Around ten minutes of continuous, fully submerged contact time in the solution.): Most quaternary ammonium salon disinfectants require roughly ten minutes of continuous, fully submerged contact time before an implement is considered disinfected. This figure comes directly from the manufacturer's label, and a stylist follows the specific product's instructions rather than a generic guess. Pulling a tool out earlier than the stated time breaks the contact period and does not achieve the intended result. Excessively long soaking is unnecessary and can damage some metal tools over repeated use.
  51. Reaching for a stronger volume at this point could push the hair beyond the level already achieved. Matching developer strength to the specific goal of each step, not just the overall service, is good formulation practice.

Continuing to apply pressure indefinitely without reassessment is not an appropriate substitute for seeking proper medical care. A topical product meant for an unrelated service is not part of basic salon first aid for an open wound. Recognizing when an injury exceeds the scope of salon first aid, and referring appropriately, is the correct response.
  56. An accurate current estimate guides how much natural or natural-warm base the new formula needs. Using stale information risks an uneven or insufficient coverage result.
  57. The correct answer is C (The elevation, which shapes the section being cut.): The cutting comb's angle relative to the head directly sets the elevation, and therefore the shape being cut, since elevation is the angle at which the subsection is held away from the head. Comb angle has no relationship to colour formulation, clipper guard branding, or a client's natural texture. Controlling this angle consistently is central to a repeatable cutting result.
  58. The correct answer is D (A leave-in conditioner or protein treatment to help replace moisture and support structure.): A leave-in conditioner or protein treatment is commonly applied after the neutralizing shampoo to help replace moisture and support the hair's structure, addressing the fact that relaxed hair is more porous and needs additional support between salon visits. A second application of relaxer would add unnecessary and risky chemical exposure rather than support the hair. Repeating neutralizing shampoo multiple times is not the standard approach to this concern, and stripping moisture with a strong clarifying shampoo works against the goal of supporting porous, relaxed hair.
  59. The correct answer is B (Repeated passes multiply damage more than one slightly higher single pass.): Repeated passes of a hot tool over the same section multiply the risk of damage far more than a single slower pass at a slightly higher temperature. Working in clean, appropriately sized sections and moving smoothly in one pass is safer than dragging a tool back and forth. This is why one controlled pass is preferred over several quick strokes through the same section.
  60. Disregarding home applied colour on the assumption that only professional services count leaves an important gap in the client's actual chemical history. Assuming a boxed product has already faded is a guess rather than a confirmed fact and should be checked through a strand test if there is any real doubt. Recording this detail before planning the next service is part of a proper consultation.

This kind of undertone matching is a standard part of a thorough colour consultation. Level alone does not address this consideration, since it says nothing about warmth or coolness.
  62. Introducing it before the base shape is set would make it difficult to judge how much texture is actually needed. This sequencing mirrors how point cutting and texturizing are also used as finishing steps.
  63. The correct answer is B (The hair's fine texture, since finer hair generally processes faster than coarse hair.): Hair texture is a genuine factor in perm chemistry, since fine hair has less protein structure for the solution to work through and generally processes faster than coarse hair, which affects both product strength and timing decisions. Shoe size has no physiological connection to hair texture. Retail promotions and appointment scheduling are business considerations with no bearing on how a specific client's hair will chemically respond to a perm solution.
  64. A regular gentle shampoo and a leave-in conditioner will not break down the silicone coating left on the shaft. A clarifying shampoo lifts the accumulated film so the hair can absorb moisture again.
  65.   66. It is brought up to the scalp last, where body heat speeds development. This sequence keeps the finished level even from root to end though the hair was not treated at once.

A one-length blunt cut would not add any interior volume at all. Skin fades and clipper-over-comb work are unrelated techniques suited to close, graduated perimeter styling rather than adding interior lift to longer hair.
  68. Testing after the service would defeat its purpose of preventing a reaction in the first place. Reactions do not reliably appear within minutes, which is why same-day testing right before a service is not the standard approach, and a full week is longer than the generally used window.
  69. This loss of elasticity is a subtle but important sign of thermal damage. It signals a need for heat protectant and lower styling temperatures.
  70. Raising a hazard such as a frayed cord at a staff meeting is a direct example of exercising this right, and the employer has a duty to respond rather than dismiss the concern. Lunch break timing, client assignment, and bonus structure are workplace policy matters unrelated to this specific health and safety right. With the head split into uneven blocks, some sections receive more product and more processing time than the hair beside them. Clean, even partings let the stylist control saturation and timing consistently.
  72. The correct answer is D (Too much bulk was removed from hair that already had low density.): Fine, low-density hair has less bulk to begin with, so aggressive point cutting can remove too much and leave the ends looking thin or stringy rather than softly textured. Adjusting how much is removed, or skipping heavy texturizing on very fine hair, prevents this outcome. The amount of bulk removed should always match the hair's existing density.
  73. The correct answer is C (Residual alkaline chemical and pH imbalance may not be fully addressed on the hair.): Neutralizing shampoo needs its full recommended contact time to properly address residual alkaline chemical and help stabilize the hair's pH, so rinsing it away almost immediately risks leaving that job incomplete. It does not work fully on first contact regardless of timing. This step does not control curl or reverse straightness, since the relaxer's own chemistry already handles the actual straightening, and neutralizing shampoo's role is chemical stabilization rather than shaping the hair.
  74. The correct answer is A (Gel, which dries into a firm coating as the water evaporates.): Gel is typically a water-based formula built around a film-forming polymer, and as the water evaporates the polymer dries into a firm, sometimes hard, coating that gives strong hold. This makes gel a good choice for slicked-back styles or for locking a curl clump into its defined shape. A gel set often dries with a stiff cast meant to be broken up by hand.
  75. The correct answer is C (Use a surface disinfectant on the bowl and chair, and sterilize blood risk implements separately.): Hard, non-porous surfaces such as a shampoo bowl or chair are cleaned and disinfected with a surface appropriate product between clients. Implements that draw blood or carry a real risk of blood contact require the higher standard of sterilization rather than surface disinfection or wet immersion. Treating every surface and implement identically ignores the different risk levels each category presents. Choosing the correct process for each category is part of running a compliant salon.
  76. Planning for these specific stages allows the stylist to select an appropriate toning strategy in advance. Assuming no warmth will appear would leave the stylist unprepared for the actual result.

Clipper guards alone would not produce the movement and softness the client wants. The choice of tool follows the desired shape rather than a strict gender-based rule.
  78. Natural hair colour shade has no established connection to checking whether hair has been overprocessed.
  79. The correct answer is A (Blow air from the ends back toward the scalp to lift the cuticle.): Reversing the air direction, blowing from the ends back toward the scalp, lifts the cuticle and roughens the surface, which is sometimes done deliberately to add texture or volume. It is the wrong technique when the goal is a sleek finish. Direction of airflow determines whether the result is sleek or textured.
  80. The correct answer is B (Storing flammable aerosol products near a heat source raises a real fire risk.): Flammable aerosol products should be stored away from heat sources, and stacking them directly on a space heater creates a real fire risk regardless of how neatly the display is arranged. The heater being turned off overnight does not address the risk during the hours it is actively running and generating heat near the aerosols. Aerosol products carry fire risk whether sealed or already opened, since the flammable propellant inside is present either way. Relocating the display away from any heat source directly addresses this specific hazard.
  81. The correct answer is B (Blend in a small amount of a warm, gold or copper based pigment.): Adding a small amount of a warm gold or copper pigment shifts an overly ashy, flat result toward a more balanced, less cool appearance. Warm tones sit on the opposite side of the wheel from ash and violet, so a measured warm addition softens the cool overcast. Adding more of the same ash pigment would only deepen the original problem.
  82. The correct answer is D (Following the natural upper boundary where beard growth thins out.): The cheek line is typically cut to follow the natural upper boundary where the beard growth thins out, rather than an artificial high line that looks disconnected from the client's own pattern. Placing it well above that boundary would look unnatural on most clients. Working with the natural growth pattern produces a more intentional, cohesive result.
  83. The correct answer is B (Roughly 13 to 14, making lye relaxers highly alkaline and fast acting.): Sodium hydroxide relaxers generally sit in a pH range of roughly 13 to 14, which makes them highly alkaline and allows them to work quickly, producing very straight, long-lasting results. This high alkalinity is also why they can cause scalp irritation or chemical burns if left in contact with skin, which is why a protective base cream is applied first. The other pH ranges listed do not reflect the strongly alkaline chemistry that defines a lye relaxer.
  84. The correct answer is C (A strong, firm hold meant to withstand humidity and long hours of wear.): Hairspray hold levels range from a light, flexible hold meant to be brushed through, up to a strong, firm hold meant to withstand humidity and hours of wear. The strong, firm category is the one most often used to finish an updo or a special occasion style. A light flexible hold would allow the updo to relax and fall before the event ends.
  85. The correct answer is A (Restart the full contact time from zero once the implement returns to the solution.): Removing an implement from the disinfecting solution before the full contact time has elapsed breaks the process, because the solution needs a continuous, uninterrupted period against the surface to work. Once the shears are pulled out early, the manufacturer's stated contact time no longer applies to the partial exposure that already occurred. The safest and correct response is to begin timing again from the moment the implement is fully resubmerged. Assuming partial credit for the interrupted time, or skipping straight to a different process, does not meet the disinfection standard.
  86. The correct answer is D (The previously lightened hair has an altered, weakened cortex that cannot safely take another full pass.): Overlapping fresh lightener onto hair that was lightened in a previous session adds further chemical action to a cortex that has already been processed, so its protein structure and bonds give way and the hair breaks. The damage comes from repeated chemical stress rather than from the absence of melanin pigment. Breakage cannot be reversed once it happens, so the stylist should respect processing times and refuse a service the hair cannot tolerate.
  87. The correct answer is C (Finer, closely spaced teeth for delicate, controlled blending.): Finer, closely spaced teeth remove less hair per stroke, which suits delicate blending on finer hair textures that would otherwise show comb marks or gaps. Wider-spaced teeth remove too much bulk per pass for this purpose. A straight beveled edge and a dry razor are not appropriate tools for this specific blending task.
  88. The correct answer is C (That enough disulfide bonds have broken to form the desired curl pattern.): Checking a wrapped section during processing is how a stylist confirms the curl pattern has actually developed enough before moving to neutralizer, since processing time varies by hair and cannot be judged by a clock alone. This check is about the chemical progress of the curl itself, not about scalp irritation, rod placement, or skin staining, even though a stylist would also watch for those issues separately during the service. A stylist who watches the clock alone could miss the unevenness that only a direct look at the curl pattern reveals, which is why the section is checked rather than simply timed.
  89. The correct answer is C (Give the hair recovery time between the chemical service and heavy thermal styling.): Giving hair recovery time between a chemical service and heavy thermal styling reduces the combined stress on a shaft that is already vulnerable immediately after processing. A heat protectant helps but does not remove the need for recovery time, and a higher temperature or a clarifying shampoo would add further stress. Planning the services with a gap protects the structural integrity of the hair.
  90. The correct answer is B (Each service type carries its own realistic time window that should be estimated separately.): Each service type carries a different realistic time window, and a simple dry cut takes far less chair time than a chemical texture service, which also adds fixed processing waits of its own. Treating both services as requiring roughly the same amount of time misrepresents how differently they are structured. Assuming the more involved chemical service takes less time than the simple cut reverses the actual relationship between the two. Estimating each service on its own realistic terms is what supports an accurate, workable schedule.
  91. The correct answer is A (30 volume developer is generally suited to reach roughly three levels of lift.): 30 volume developer is generally capable of lifting the natural level by roughly three levels, making it a reasonable choice when a client wants a clearly noticeable lightening change in one visit. Choosing a weaker volume like 10 or 20 would likely fall short of this specific goal. Matching developer strength to the requested amount of lift is a core formulation skill.
  92. The correct answer is C (Elevation approaching ninety degrees, producing stronger graduation or layering.): Elevation approaching ninety degrees, where hair is held straight out from the head, produces stronger graduation or true uniform layering and removes significantly more weight, creating a lighter, more textured result. Zero degrees keeps maximum weight instead of removing it. An elevation below thirty degrees would not create this kind of significant weight removal.
  93. The correct answer is D (Hair porosity, since a raised or damaged cuticle lets solution reach the cortex faster.): Porosity is generally considered the single biggest factor in perm processing time, since a raised or damaged cuticle gives the reducing solution easier access to the cortex, allowing porous hair to process more quickly than hair with a tight, healthy cuticle. Natural hair colour, appointment time of day, and general room temperature are not established factors in how quickly hair processes chemically in the way porosity is. Because porosity controls how easily the solution can reach the cortex, a stylist adjusts processing time to the hair's actual condition rather than relying on a single fixed setting for every client.
  94. The correct answer is B (Straight or chemically relaxed hair that holds a defined S-shape.): Finger waving works best on hair with enough length and a texture that will hold the S-shape, typically straight or chemically relaxed hair. Naturally curly or coily hair will not mold into the flat wave pattern in the same predictable way. The technique builds precise finger control and directional combing skill.
  95. The correct answer is D (Set the comb aside for sterilization and use a different tool while it is processed.): A tool that has contacted a client's blood needs the higher standard of sterilization rather than routine disinfection, because standard wet immersion is not designed to destroy bacterial spores at the level a blood exposed implement requires. Being busy does not lower the required standard, so the correct response is to set that specific comb aside for proper sterilization and continue serving other clients with a different, already processed tool. Visual cleanliness is not a reliable indicator that a tool is safe to reuse after blood contact. Rescheduling every waiting client is unnecessary when other clean implements remain available.
  96. The correct answer is D (Explore a different tested product line or non-oxidative options that still meet the goals.): A positive predisposition test rules out the original oxidative product for this client regardless of the upcoming event, so the responsible path is to look for a different product line the client has not reacted to, retested appropriately, or a non-oxidative option that can still move toward lighter, blended results even if it cannot fully replicate the original plan. This means adjusting the lightening and grey blending goals to fit what is actually safe, rather than forcing the original formula through under time pressure. Balancing client safety against a real scheduling deadline and a cosmetic preference for blended grey is exactly the kind of layered judgment call this scenario is built to test.
  97. The correct answer is A (Define clean edges along the cheek line, neckline, and moustache border.): After bulk length is set with a guard, the edges are refined using clippers or a trimmer without a guard to define clean lines along the cheek line, neckline, and moustache border. Skipping this step would leave the beard looking unfinished despite the even overall length. Edge definition is what gives the trim its finished, intentional appearance.
  98. The correct answer is A (Larger-diameter rods with moderate, even tension throughout the wrapped sections.): Larger-diameter rods produce a looser, wider wave rather than a tight curl, which matches this client's stated preference, and moderate, even tension supports a uniform result across the head. Small rods with extra tension would move the result toward tighter, more defined curls, which is the opposite of what the client asked for. Tightly wrapping only near the scalp creates an uneven pattern rather than the soft, consistent wave requested, and shortening processing time changes how developed the curl is, not its overall looseness.
  99. The correct answer is C (A strong, firm hold spray meant to withstand humidity and hours of wear.): Hairspray hold levels range from a light, flexible hold meant to be brushed through and allow natural movement up to a strong, firm hold meant to withstand humidity and hours of wear. A strong, firm hold is the category most often used to finish an updo or a special occasion style. A light spray or a serum would not hold the style through a full day of wear, and mousse is used during styling rather than as a finishing product.
  100. The correct answer is D (This is a professional conduct concern even without any formal complaint being filed.): Continuing to meet the sanitation and safety standards a licence was granted under is part of professional conduct, and letting those standards slip is a concern even without a formal complaint being filed. Assuming the absence of a complaint means there is no issue overlooks that professional standards apply on an ongoing basis, not only when someone happens to notice and report a problem. These expectations continue well beyond the original certification exam itself. Sanitation practice is squarely part of professional conduct, not a separate personal matter.
  101. The correct answer is C (A darker band along the line where the new colour overlapped old colour.): Applying full strength colour over previously coloured hair is a leading cause of visible banding and of colour that looks darker than intended. The overlap deposits extra pigment onto sections that already hold colour. A careful retouch stays off the old colour or blends only the last half inch onto it lightly near the end of processing.
  102. The correct answer is C (Proper disinfection according to provincial infection control standards.): Any tool that contacts the skin, including clippers, must be cleaned of visible debris and properly disinfected between every client according to provincial infection control standards, since bloodborne pathogen exposure is a real risk. Wiping off visible hair alone does not meet this disinfection requirement. Guard size, tool switching, and blade speed do not address the infection control gap.
  103. The correct answer is B (It provides a clear record of what was tested and observed, guiding a safe next step.): A written log of predisposition test results, including the date, product, and outcome, gives the stylist a clear record to refer back to if a client later reports a delayed reaction, helping guide an appropriate and informed next step. This kind of record remains useful well after a service is completed, contrary to treating it as no longer relevant. Its purpose is client safety and continuity of care, not retail tracking, and a delayed report is exactly the situation where having documented results in advance matters most.
  104. The correct answer is D (There was too little tension, leaving the cuticle rough and the hair less shiny.): Insufficient tension leaves the cuticle rough and the finished hair less shiny, because consistent tension is what turns a wet section into a smooth, controlled one. The stylist should maintain enough tension to control the section without overloading fragile hair. A rough, dull finish after a blow-dry often points back to tension and airflow direction.
  105. The correct answer is A (A new client with a more involved service needs added time for consultation and assessment.): A new or unfamiliar client, especially one requesting a more involved service such as a colour correction, needs extra time for a thorough consultation and any required testing that an established client's routine touch up would not need. Treating every appointment length as identical regardless of these differences overlooks how much the required steps actually vary. A colour correction is typically a more time intensive service, not a shorter one, compared with a routine touch up. Recognizing this difference when booking helps avoid a rushed, underprepared appointment.
  106. The correct answer is B (Temporary or semi-permanent colour is the gentlest option for this client's hair.): Temporary and semi-permanent colours are the gentlest categories available because they deposit pigment without requiring an oxidative chemical reaction, making them appropriate for fine or previously compromised hair. Reaching for a permanent or high lift service would add unnecessary chemical stress to already fragile hair. Matching product gentleness to hair condition is a basic professional responsibility.
  107. The correct answer is D (More weight is removed than the client actually wanted.): Elevation approaching ninety degrees removes significantly more weight and builds graduation, so using it when the client wanted a solid, weight-heavy perimeter would remove more weight than intended rather than preserving it. It would not make the perimeter heavier, since higher elevation reduces perimeter density. Elevation does not correct itself once the cut is made.
  108. The correct answer is A (The crown sections were wrapped with smaller, more precise subsections than the nape.): Smaller subsections wrapped with more rods generally produce a more defined, uniform curl because the hair is held more precisely against the rod surface, so a difference in subsection size between the crown and nape, even with the same rod diameter, can account for a difference in definition. There is no basis for the crown rejecting solution differently, curl definition is not random, and a doubled neutralizer time on only one area would be an application error rather than the underlying explanation here. Once the neutralizer has been applied, the bonds lock into whatever shape the hair is already in, so differences in how tightly the hair was held during the wrap are what show up as different definition between the crown and nape.
  109. The correct answer is D (The irregular shaft gives sebum a harder path to travel down the length.): Curly and coily hair shafts are irregular rather than round, so natural sebum produced at the scalp has a harder path to travel down the length of the hair. This leaves these textures naturally drier than straighter textures and makes moisture-focused products a core part of styling. The scalp still produces sebum, and it is the shape of the shaft rather than the amount of oil that drives the difference.
  110. The correct answer is B (Insufficiently lubricated blades increase friction, which can create a pulling sensation during cutting.): Insufficiently lubricated clipper blades create more friction against the hair, which can produce the pulling sensation the client is describing rather than a smooth, clean cutting action. Dismissing any connection between lubrication and how a clipper feels contradicts how these blades actually function. While hair type can influence the experience, it does not explain a pulling sensation that follows a known gap in the clipper's maintenance routine. Restoring the clipper's regular oiling schedule is the practical fix that connects to this specific complaint.
  111. The correct answer is B (Red, yellow, and blue are the three primary colours on the wheel.): Red, yellow, and blue cannot be created by mixing other colours, which is why colour theory classifies them as primary. Every secondary and tertiary colour on the wheel is built from combinations of these three. This principle underlies how a colourist predicts and neutralizes tone in a formula.
  112. The correct answer is A (Whether the nape and both sides fall evenly relative to each other.): Checking a finished cut from directly behind the client is specifically meant to reveal whether the nape and both sides fall evenly relative to one another, an angle that is not always visible from the front. This is part of the standard multi-angle balance check used to finish any haircut. Teaching an apprentice to use this specific angle builds a habit that catches errors other views might miss.
  113. The correct answer is A (Stop and move to neutralizing or rinsing right away rather than continuing as planned.): A mushy, gummy, almost gelatinous feel while wet is one of the earliest and most reliable signs that processing has gone too far, so a stylist who notices this should stop and move to neutralizing or rinsing immediately rather than continuing on the planned schedule. This texture is not a normal, expected part of every perm. Adding more reducing solution would worsen an already overprocessed situation. Waiting further risks allowing more damage to occur rather than addressing what has already been observed.
  114. The correct answer is C (A rigid, ridged perm rod, which creates firm separated curls on undamaged wet hair.): Perm rods come in the same diameter range as rollers but have a more rigid, ridged construction. They are commonly associated with chemical perming but are also used on wet, undamaged hair to create a firm, well-defined curl pattern. On coily hair a rod set can produce clearly separated, springy curls that a standard roller set would not achieve as reliably, and large rollers would only give a looser, less defined result.
  115. The correct answer is C (Ask further questions about the client's hair history, lifestyle, and maintenance expectations first.): A consultation begins with active listening about what the client wants, their lifestyle, maintenance willingness, and history with previous services, rather than assuming a photo alone communicates everything needed. Starting immediately from a photo skips the physical assessment that determines whether the desired result is even safe or achievable on this client's actual hair. Declining outright is an overcorrection when the real issue is simply an incomplete consultation, not the photo itself. Gathering the fuller picture before proceeding protects both the client's hair and the outcome of the service.
  116. The correct answer is B (It redeposits missing pigment and evens porosity so the next colour grabs evenly.): A colour filler is applied to porous, damaged hair to redeposit missing pigment and even out porosity along the strand, filling the gaps left by lightening so the hair accepts the next colour uniformly. It is chosen in a level and tone that restores what the natural pigment would have contributed, most often a warm gold or copper base. Skipping this step and going straight to the target shade often gives a patchy result that fades unevenly.
  117. The correct answer is D (Elevate interior crown sections higher while keeping the perimeter length intact.): Raising elevation on the interior crown sections builds lift and movement there while the perimeter length, left at lower elevation, stays close to the client's requested overall length. Cutting the whole head at zero elevation would remove the layering effect entirely. Graduation would shorten and stack the perimeter rather than build interior lift.
  118. The correct answer is D (The second visit may have actually overlapped relaxer onto the first service's treated hair.): A true virgin application should only happen once, on hair that has never been chemically straightened before, so a record showing two virgin applications six months apart raises the concern that the second visit likely overlapped fresh relaxer onto hair already treated at the first visit, which carries a real breakage risk. This pattern is not normal and deserves attention rather than being dismissed. Virgin applications are not repeated by default once hair has already been treated, and the labelling matters directly to how safely the next service should be planned.
  119. The correct answer is C (A moderate range above the range for fine damaged hair.): Hair of average texture and condition sits in a moderate range above the lowest range that fine, damaged, or chemically processed hair tolerates. Coarse, resistant, or virgin hair can generally tolerate the highest range. When in doubt the stylist starts at the lowest temperature that will realistically achieve the result and raises it only if needed.
  120. The correct answer is C (It documents informed consent, protecting both the client's choice and the stylist's standard of care.): A consent form documents that the client was informed of the risks, that a patch test was offered or performed, and that they agreed to proceed, protecting the client's ability to make an informed choice while also showing the stylist followed a reasonable standard of care. It is not a payment collection tool, and it applies specifically to services carrying a meaningful chemical risk rather than services with no chemical involved. It does not replace the consultation or assessment, which still need to happen alongside it. Its purpose is documentation of informed agreement, not a substitute for the earlier steps.
  121. The correct answer is B (Violet and orange are not complementary, so the toner leaves a muddy tone.): Violet neutralizes yellow because violet and yellow are complementary. Orange sits with blue on the wheel, so violet and orange are not complementary and will not fully cancel. Toning still-orange hair with violet leaves a muddy tone instead of a clean neutral.
  122. The correct answer is A (Razor cutting can weaken curly strands and increase split ends and frizz.): The diagonal razor cut increases the surface area of each strand at the cutting point, which weakens already fragile curl patterns and raises the risk of split ends and frizz. This is why razor cutting is generally avoided on very curly or coily textures. Explaining the trade-off helps the client choose a technique better suited to her hair type.
  123. The correct answer is B (The neutralizer was rinsed out before it had time to fully reform the bonds.): If neutralizer is rinsed too early, some disulfide bonds never get the chance to reform in the new curled position, leaving the curl weak and prone to dropping quickly. Overprocessing with the reducing solution tends to cause damage and breakage rather than a curl that simply drops. Rod size and wrapping tension change the shape and definition of the curl, not whether the bonds actually reformed. The specific pattern described points directly to incomplete neutralization.
  124. The correct answer is C (The French twist, built by rolling and twisting the length upward against a vertical seam.): The French twist is built by smoothing the hair to one side across the back of the head, then rolling and twisting the length upward against itself along a vertical seam and pinning it in place. It produces a classic, elongated silhouette that is a frequent choice for formal styling. A chignon is a low gathered style at the nape, and a braid is woven, so neither produces the same vertical seam.
  125. The correct answer is A (Confirming the client's satisfaction with the result before they leave the chair.): Confirming satisfaction before the client leaves the chair closes the consultation loop and reduces the chance of a dispute after the fact, and it is a distinct final step from the earlier stages of the process. Chemical history review, pricing discussion, and scalp assessment are all separate parts of the consultation that would typically occur earlier in the appointment. Skipping the final check-in misses a chance to catch and address any concern while the client is still present. Making this a consistent habit closes out the service properly.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (The amount of contrast and the width of the transition zone from root to end.): Ombre describes a finished look with a strong, deliberate transition from a darker base near the root to a noticeably lighter end, normally achieved with balayage, foiling, or a combination of both. What defines ombre is the amount of contrast and the width of the transition zone, not the tool used. A stylist cannot call a result ombre just because they used one technique.
  2. The correct answer is B (Visible steps or ridges appearing in the graduated line being cut.): Unsteady pressure or angle while guiding the comb produces inconsistent cutting depth along the section, which appears as visible steps or ridges once the pass is complete. A steady guiding hand is one of the core requirements for smooth clipper-over-comb work. Practicing controlled, even pressure prevents this common error.
  3. The correct answer is C (The full head processing time should be shortened to match what the test curl showed.): A test curl exists precisely to reveal how a specific client's hair actually processes, so when it forms fully faster than the standard estimate, the stylist should shorten the planned processing time for the full head to avoid overprocessing the rest of the hair. Sticking with the standard time despite clear evidence to the contrary defeats the purpose of running the test. The result reflects the client's hair broadly, not just that isolated section, and switching to a stronger agent would work against a result that already shows faster than average processing.
  4. The correct answer is A (A light flexible hold meant to allow natural movement.): Hairspray hold levels range from a light, flexible hold meant to be brushed through and allow natural movement, up to a strong, firm hold meant to withstand humidity and hours of wear. The strong hold category is most often used to finish an updo or special occasion style. Matching the hold to the desired movement is the goal.
  5. The correct answer is B (In the product's safety data sheet, which follows a standardized sixteen section format.): Safety data sheets follow a standardized sixteen section format that includes identification, hazard information, composition, first aid measures, handling, storage, and exposure controls for a specific product. Employers must keep these sheets accessible to every worker, and stylists are expected to know where they are kept and how to read the relevant sections. A dress code handbook, a one time verbal explanation, or a shipping invoice do not meet this requirement and are not a substitute for the actual data sheet. The safety data sheet is the correct and legally required source for this level of detail.
  6. The correct answer is D (Each foil packet is independent, so one service can be timed separately from the rest.): Foil packets let a stylist run more than one formula in the same head at the same time, because each foil is independent of the others. A lightener foil near the crown can be checked and removed while a gentler foil elsewhere continues processing. This control makes foiling the standard technique for precise, repeatable placement.
  7. The correct answer is D (A cutting line and volume distribution that draws focus away from the forehead.): Basic face shape analysis is used to plan a cutting line and elevation strategy that suits the client's individual proportions, so for a wider forehead relative to the jaw, a strategy that draws focus away from the forehead is consistent with this general approach. Adding maximum height and volume directly at the forehead would tend to emphasize that width rather than balance it. Ignoring the client's proportions entirely defeats the purpose of the consultation step described in this chapter.
  8. The correct answer is C (Work carefully up to the line without deliberately extending product onto treated hair.): The correct technique at a retouch's growth line is to work carefully up to it without deliberately extending product onto the previously treated hair, since that treated hair does not need or benefit from another application and overlapping it risks breakage. Applying product generously past the line is the overlap error the technique is specifically meant to avoid. Skipping the area right at the line entirely can leave a visible band of underprocessed hair. Using a stronger relaxer at the line does not address the underlying overlap risk and adds unnecessary chemical strength.
  9. The correct answer is B (It lets the stylist and client agree on the shape and confirm the style holds.): A styling trial before the event date is standard practice for bridal work, since it lets the stylist and client agree on the shape and confirm the style holds up over time. It also allows timing to be coordinated with other vendors. The trial is about verifying the result rather than setting price or booking.
  10. The correct answer is A (The tool requires full sterilization in an autoclave before its next use.): An extraction tool that contacts skin and draws fluid carries a real risk of blood exposure, which places it in the sterilization category rather than routine disinfection. Sterilization in an autoclave destroys all forms of microbial life, including bacterial spores, which a wipe or a brief rinse cannot achieve. A surface disinfectant intended for hard salon surfaces is not designed to meet the sterilization standard this tool requires. Treating this tool the same as a comb or a shear would leave a genuine infection risk unaddressed.
  11. The correct answer is D (Toner deposits colour, so it cannot lift the under lightened hair to ash.): Toner deposits colour, it does not lift. If the hair did not lift to a high enough level to support the intended tone, no toner can turn genuinely under lightened orange hair into a true ash blonde. The stylist must first decide whether further lightening is safe for the condition of the hair.
  12. The correct answer is C (The curl pattern contracts as it dries, shrinking the visible length.): Hair shrinkage on drying is a critical consideration for curly and coily textures, where the strand can shrink dramatically in visible length as it dries and the curl pattern contracts. This is a texture effect, not a sign of dull shears or an actual over-removal of length during the cut. Length is still measured the same way on wet or dry hair, it simply appears shorter once the curl contracts.
  13. The correct answer is A (Perform a test curl on a small section using the chosen solution and rod.): Uncertain chemical history is exactly the situation a test curl is meant to address, since it gives the stylist direct evidence of how this hair responds to the chosen product rather than relying on a guess. Proceeding straight to a full service without any check risks a poor or damaging result on hair whose history is unknown. Uncertain history genuinely does affect how hair processes, and asking the client to guess does not provide reliable information for planning a safe service.
  14. The correct answer is C (Wash with a clarifying, sulfate-based shampoo to fully remove the build-up.): Most silicones used in finishing products are not water soluble, so a build-up of product over repeated use typically needs a clarifying, sulfate-based shampoo to fully remove. A regular gentle shampoo leaves the silicone coating behind, and a conditioner or leave-in adds more product rather than removing it. A clarifying wash gives the hair a clean slate before the next styling.
  15. The correct answer is A (A government inspector is brought in to review the situation and make a final call.): When a refusal is not resolved through the supervisor and investigation stages, the established process allows a government inspector to step in and make a final determination on whether the task or equipment is genuinely unsafe. The worker is not simply required to defer to the employer's opinion, and the matter is not settled by a staff vote or by default in the employer's favour. This escalation step is what protects a worker's right to refuse from being overridden without independent review. It exists precisely for situations where the initial parties cannot agree.
  16. The correct answer is B (A warm gold or copper base that restores the hair's natural contribution.): Fillers are typically chosen in a level and tone that restores what the hair's natural pigment would have contributed, most often a warm gold or copper base. This redeposits the missing pigment and evens porosity along the strand. It prepares the hair to accept the next colour uniformly.
  17. The correct answer is A (The shortest guard, or bare skin, at the very lowest point of the fade.): A skin fade begins at bare skin at the lowest point, typically along the hairline or temple, before the stylist works upward through progressively longer guards. Starting with the longest guard at the hairline would not create the graduated bald-to-longer effect the technique is known for. The starting point at bare skin is what distinguishes a skin fade from a standard taper.
  18. The correct answer is A (Maintain the base cream application and freshness check despite the pressure to move faster.): Time pressure does not change the underlying safety reasons for the base cream application, which protects the scalp from a highly alkaline product, or the freshness check, which confirms the product will perform as tested, so the best response is maintaining both despite the schedule pressure. Treating either step as expendable to save time introduces a real risk of scalp injury or unpredictable chemical performance. Choosing to drop one step over the other still accepts an avoidable risk that maintaining both steps would have prevented entirely.
  19. The correct answer is D (A curl defining cream applied after the leave in conditioner.): A curl-defining cream is generally heavier and richer than a leave-in conditioner and carries some light styling hold. It is applied afterward to coat individual curl clumps, encourage them to stay grouped together, and reduce frizz along the surface of the curl. The leave-in handles moisture and slip while the cream defines the shape.
  20. The correct answer is D (PPE should be selected task by task, adding chemical protection only for the lightening service.): PPE is matched to the specific task rather than applied uniformly across an entire day, so chemical resistant gloves and any additional protection are appropriate for the lightening service but not necessarily for the dry cut or blow dry. Wearing the same chemical gloves through every service, including tasks with no chemical exposure, does not reflect correct task based selection. Assuming no PPE is needed simply because most of the day is non chemical ignores the one service that does require it. Reassessing PPE needs as the task changes throughout the day is the correct habit.
  21. The correct answer is C (The hair can look drab or muddy rather than a clean, neutral shade.): Using more complementary pigment than needed does not simply erase colour, it pushes the result toward the opposite unwanted cast, which can read as drab, muddy, or flat. Neutralization is a balancing act between two opposing tones, not a one way removal process. Careful, measured application avoids trading one problem for another.
  22. The correct answer is B (A horizontal cutting line, kept level and parallel to the floor.): A horizontal cutting line runs parallel to the floor and is used to build even, blunt shapes such as a straight-across fringe with no angled framing. A diagonal forward line instead produces a face-framing effect with progressively longer hair. Diagonal back and vertical lines each move length in a direction unrelated to a level, straight-across fringe.
  23. The correct answer is B (A virgin application covers the entire length, while a retouch covers only new growth.): A virgin application is the first relaxer service on hair that has never been chemically straightened before, so it is applied to the entire length from roots to ends. A retouch is applied only to the new growth that has come in since the last service, since the previously straightened hair does not need or benefit from another full application. The reversed description and the claim that both always cover the full length or only the scalp do not reflect how these two applications actually differ.
  24. The correct answer is B (A moderate range, above the lowest setting and below the highest used for coarse hair.): Tool temperature has to be matched to the hair's condition and texture rather than set to one number for every client. Average texture hair in healthy condition sits in a moderate range above the setting used for fine or damaged hair and below the range for coarse, resistant, or virgin hair. Starting at the lowest temperature that will realistically achieve the result and raising it only if needed keeps the heat exposure minimal.
  25. Declining until proper training is provided is a reasonable and protected response, not a violation of workplace expectations. Asking a client, or improvising based on an unrelated tool, introduces unnecessary risk rather than resolving the actual gap in training. The correct path is requesting training before the equipment is used.

Working down from the root in smaller sections lets each band process for a shorter time than the one above it. Feathering the timing this way softens the multiple lines gradually.
  27.   28. The correct answer is C (It oxidizes the sulfur groups so the disulfide bonds reform in the new curled shape.): The neutralizer, usually a hydrogen peroxide solution, adds oxygen back to the sulfur groups that were separated by the reducing agent, which reforms the disulfide bonds. Because the hair is still wrapped around the rod at this point, the bonds lock into the new curled position rather than the original straight shape. Colour, residue removal, and porosity are not the neutralizer's chemical function in a perm service. Its role is specifically to rebuild the internal bonds that create the lasting curl.
  29. The correct answer is C (Silicone, which coats the shaft and seals down the cuticle to reduce frizz.): Silicone, often listed under names such as dimethicone, is responsible for the smoothing effect of a finishing serum. Silicones coat the outside of the hair shaft, sealing down the cuticle and filling in surface irregularities, which reduces frizz, adds shine, and lowers porosity. Because most silicones used in these products are not water soluble, product build-up over repeated use needs a clarifying, sulfate-based shampoo to remove.

Humidity from shampoo services, tools stored together in a drawer, and lightweight cotton capes are not recognized fire safety hazards in this context. Knowing which factors actually raise fire risk allows a stylist to address the real hazards rather than unrelated details. Overloaded outlets and improperly stored aerosols are the specific concerns fire safety practice in a salon addresses.

Cleaning only once at day's end is the opposite of the recommended habit, and cleaning frequency does have a real, measurable effect on the tool's condition over time. Heavier daily use is actually a reason for more frequent cleaning, not less. Wiping the shears after every client protects both cutting performance and the tool's working life.

  65. Sodium hydroxide alone identifies a lye relaxer instead. Ammonium thioglycolate identifies a thio relaxer built on reducing chemistry rather than hydroxide chemistry. Hydrogen peroxide is associated with oxidizing and neutralizing processes rather than being the primary straightening agent in any of these three relaxer types.

It is distinct from banding, which is a single visible line at the transition. It is prevented by disciplined retouch application that stays off previously coloured hair, and corrected with a colour reducer or bleach bath.

The two components are chemically distinct rather than identical, and mixing early would allow the reaction to proceed and potentially weaken by the time it is applied, rather than strengthening it.
