Extending it higher would look inconsistent with the surrounding growth. Working with the client's actual growth pattern is standard practice for a natural finish.
  93. The correct answer is D (The stylist likely applied relaxer accurately to the new growth without overlapping treated hair.): A smooth match in texture between the new growth and the previously relaxed lengths, without a visible demarcation line, generally indicates the stylist applied relaxer accurately to the new growth and blended the timing well, without significant overlap onto already-treated hair. This is the sign of correct technique, not overlap, and it does not indicate the product was left on too long. This kind of smooth result is achievable specifically through a well-executed retouch, not only through a virgin application.
  94. The correct answer is C (Match the toner base to the pigment actually present, not the expected level.): Choosing the wrong toner base for the pigment actually present in the hair, rather than the level the stylist expected, is one of the most frequently tested corrective colour concepts. It requires reading the hair in front of them rather than following a formula from memory. If the warmth is yellow, a violet toner is right, but if gold or orange is still strong, the base must change.
  95. The correct answer is B (The guideline in that area drifted from the original reference points during cutting.): Localized rounding in an otherwise sharp square perimeter typically results from the guideline drifting away from the original reference points in that specific area during cutting, rather than any change in the client's hair growth. Frequent cross-checking against the original guide throughout the cut is what prevents this kind of isolated drift. Identifying and correcting the affected corner restores the intended geometric shape.
  96. The correct answer is C (A basic stacked wrap, since rows follow the head's curvature in stacked sections.): A basic stacked wrap uses rows that follow the curvature of the head, with rods stacked on top of one another in each row, which suits a client asking for curl that follows the head's natural shape rather than an elongated, angled effect. A spiral wrap produces the opposite look, an elongated curl running along the hair's full length. Rods are the physical mould that gives perm solution a shape to lock into, and wrapping pattern is a deliberate choice that directly shapes the outcome.
  97. The correct answer is A (The client's colour history and any recent chemical services performed.): A thorough consultation reviews and documents the client's colour history and any recent chemical services, since these details can affect how the hair responds to a new formula. This information, together with the predisposition test result, gives a complete picture before formulating begins. Skipping this review risks missing a contraindication that could affect the outcome or the client's safety.
  98. The correct answer is C (Perform a dry cutting check and adjust the finished shape.): Dry cutting is used deliberately to check and adjust the finished shape once the style has been blow-dried, since some textures look different once the hair has settled into its natural fall. Re-wetting the head is unnecessary for this specific check. Assuming the wet-cut shape is automatically correct, or skipping the check entirely, ignores that drying genuinely changes how a cut reads.
  99. The correct answer is C (It should confirm how this specific hair, altered by the prior treatment, responds to perm chemistry.): A prior chemical smoothing service can change how hair responds to a later chemical process, so a test curl here plays its usual role, giving the stylist direct evidence of how this specific, previously treated hair reacts to the chosen perm solution and rod. Keratin treatments can have a lasting effect on the hair's structure and response to further chemical services, so assuming no effect or identical processing to untreated hair is not a safe assumption. A test curl is valuable here independent of whether an allergy is suspected.
  100. The correct answer is B (Refuse the service and recommend protein treatments to manage the breakage.): Breakage from overprocessing happens when the hair's protein structure is pushed past what it can tolerate, often from overlapping lightener sessions. Breakage cannot be reversed once it happens; it can only be managed with protein treatments and prevented by respecting processing times and refusing a service the hair cannot tolerate. A stylist must decline a chemical service that the hair cannot safely handle.
  101. The correct answer is D (Strong graduation or layering shaped by an even, level cutting line.): Ninety degrees of elevation builds strong graduation or layering, and the horizontal cutting line shapes that result into an even, level line across the section, since cutting line and elevation work together rather than one canceling the other. It would not resemble the blunt, perimeter-heavy result of zero-degree elevation. Elevation and cutting line both meaningfully affect the outcome, so neither can be ignored in favour of guideline choice alone.
  102. The correct answer is A (The grey sections may need extra processing time since grey hair often resists solution more.): Grey hair is frequently more resistant to chemical solution than pigmented hair on the same head, often because it tends to have a more tightly packed cuticle, which can mean the grey sections need extra time to process fully while the rest of the head is already done. Assuming both sections process identically ignores this common difference. Grey hair can be permed successfully, it simply may need to be timed and checked separately from the pigmented sections.
  103. The correct answer is D (Work down in small sections and feather timing so lower bands process less.): When several retouch cycles have built up overlapping bands, the stylist may need to work down from the root in smaller sections, feathering the timing so each band processes for a shorter time than the one above it. This gradual approach softens the stacked lines instead of creating a new one. Rushing the correction with an even application over every band tends to make the problem more visible.
  104. The correct answer is D (A traveling guideline, carried forward from section to section.): A traveling guideline is carried forward from one subsection into the next, with a small piece of the previous section used as the reference point, allowing length to increase or decrease gradually around the head. A stationary guideline instead keeps the perimeter consistent rather than gradually changing. A traveling guideline works together with elevation, not in place of it.
  105. The correct answer is B (Removing remaining alkaline residue and helping stabilize the hair's pH back toward normal.): Neutralizing shampoo after a relaxer service serves two purposes together, removing remaining alkaline residue from the hair and scalp and helping stabilize the hair's pH back toward its normal, healthy range after a strongly alkaline product was used. It does not add colour pigment or seal a protein coating onto the hair. Most of the bond reformation in a relaxer service already happens as the relaxer is rinsed and the hair repositioned straight, rather than through the neutralizing shampoo step itself, and its role is not primarily to remove base cream.
  106. The correct answer is A (40 volume is typically selected, used carefully due to greater damage risk.): 40 volume developer offers the greatest lift among the four common strengths, roughly four levels, but it also carries the greatest potential for damage and scalp sensitivity, so it is used more conservatively and often with shorter processing times. Selecting this strength without added care raises the risk of compromising hair integrity. Matching strength to need while managing the added risk is the professional standard here.
  107. The correct answer is B (The apex, from which many layered cuts are measured.): The apex is the highest point on top of the head, generally located at the crown area when the head is held upright and level. Many layered and graduated cuts are measured or radiated from this point. The occipital bone, nape, and parietal ridge are all different, lower landmarks on the head.
  108. The correct answer is C (It should generally start shorter, since fine hair has less structure to process.): Fine hair has less internal protein structure than coarse hair, so the reducing solution reaches and softens it more quickly, which generally supports a shorter initial processing time estimate for the fine-haired client. Assuming fine hair resists solution more than coarse hair reverses the real relationship. Texture is a genuine and measurable factor in processing speed, and length alone does not account for how quickly the solution can act on the hair's internal structure.
  109. The correct answer is A (The two swatches carry different tone or reflect values despite sharing a level.): Two shades can share the exact same level while differing in tone, which is why one level 6 swatch can look warmer than another. Level and tone are independent variables on a professional colour chart. Comparing swatches by level alone would miss this real visible difference.
  110. The correct answer is A (The tension screw, also called the pivot screw.): The tension screw, also called the pivot screw, joins the two shear blades and controls how freely they open and close. Adjusting it correctly keeps the blades gliding smoothly without excess play or binding. The finger brace, shank, and heel are separate shear parts with different functions.
  111. The correct answer is B (Reconsider the chemical strength, timing, or overall suitability of the service for this hair.): A strand test showing visible damage and weak elasticity is exactly the kind of direct evidence that should lead a stylist to reconsider chemical strength, timing, or whether the planned service is even suitable for this specific hair. Proceeding as originally planned disregards a clear warning sign from the test. Strand tests are especially valuable on previously chemically treated hair, not limited to virgin hair, and simply repeating the identical test without any change in approach would likely just reproduce the same damaging result.
  112. The correct answer is C (A lighter percentage of natural base blended for a softer, blended effect.): A client who wants scattered grey blended rather than fully, opaquely covered is often better served by a formula using a lighter percentage of natural base tone, producing a softer blended effect rather than a flat, solid result. Using the maximum base percentage would push toward full opaque coverage, which does not match this client's stated preference. Adjusting the base percentage to the client's actual goal is part of a thorough consultation.
  113. The correct answer is B (One or both blades are notched with teeth.): Thinning shears, also called texturizing shears, have one or both blades notched with teeth rather than a single straight cutting edge. This notched design removes bulk and blends hair without shortening the overall length. A hollow-ground surface, a fixed rivet, and angled grips describe unrelated shear features.
  114. The correct answer is C (A clarifying or chelating treatment designed to remove mineral deposits from the hair.): Calcium buildup from repeated no-lye relaxer use is specifically addressed with a clarifying or chelating treatment, which is designed to lift mineral deposits out of the hair shaft. Switching to a stronger lye relaxer does not remove existing buildup and introduces an unrelated chemical change. Increasing relaxer frequency adds more chemical exposure without targeting the buildup itself, and sealing the hair with oil would trap the deposits rather than remove them.
  115. The correct answer is D (Test at each stage so the next step is based on what actually happened.): A corrective colour rarely happens in one step, and testing at each stage is what keeps it controlled. Assuming the previous step worked as planned risks compounding the original problem. This is the difference between a controlled service and one that has to be redone.
  116. The correct answer is A (Curl-by-curl cutting, assessing and cutting each curl to its own natural fall.): Curl-by-curl cutting assesses and cuts each curl or small cluster individually to its own natural fall, which accounts for curls of similar stretched length shrinking to different resting lengths. A single uniform elevation approach would not adapt to this natural variation. This technique is common on very curly and coily textures for exactly this reason.
  117. The correct answer is D (Choose a small, inconspicuous section that still uses the actual planned rod and solution.): Even on short hair, a small, inconspicuous section can usually be found that still uses the actual planned rod size and solution, preserving the value of the test without meaningfully disrupting the overall wrap pattern. Skipping the test removes the direct evidence it provides regardless of hair length. Testing with a different rod than the one actually planned would not accurately predict how the real service will behave. A cut clipping no longer reflects how hair still attached to the scalp and follicle will respond during an active service.
  118. The correct answer is C (The client may receive an unexpected warm or cool cast despite the correct level.): Ignoring the tone code means the stylist may hit the correct lightness while still producing an unexpected warm or cool result the client did not ask for. Level alone cannot describe the finished appearance of a shade. Tone must be selected deliberately alongside level to avoid this kind of mismatch.
  119. The correct answer is D (Check the blade for damage or looseness before use.): Blades should be checked for damage or looseness before use as part of routine safety practice, rather than continuing to use a tool with a known issue. Pivot looseness is not something that can be safely ignored, since it affects both cutting precision and control. Switching entirely to clippers does not address the underlying maintenance problem with the shear.
  120. The correct answer is A (No-lye formulas are generally gentler on the scalp, which suits her documented sensitivity.): The most accurate explanation ties the recommendation to the client's specific scalp sensitivity, since no-lye formulas are generally gentler on the scalp and less likely to cause the kind of burn a highly alkaline lye relaxer can. No-lye formulas are not established as more permanent than lye formulas. Lye relaxers remain a standard, available product category rather than having been discontinued, and a strand test remains a standard precaution before a first relaxer application regardless of which formula type is chosen.
  121. The correct answer is D (A demi- or semi-permanent shade near the natural level, feathered along the edge.): Root smudging, or root shadowing, uses a demi-permanent or semi-permanent shade close to the client's natural level to soften where colour meets regrowth. Feathered into the first inch or two below the root, it removes the hard demarcation. Doing it at the same visit makes the grow-out look planned.
  122. The correct answer is B (Wet hair lies close to its true, relaxed length for straight textures.): Wet hair lies close to its true, relaxed length for straight and wavy textures, which makes it easier to establish an accurate guideline without a separate shrinkage adjustment. This is not true for coily hair, where the strand can shrink dramatically as the curl pattern contracts on drying. It is not accurate that straight hair never benefits from any check, or that wet cutting only functions on straight textures.
  123. The correct answer is C (A violet-based toner, which sits opposite yellow and cancels it on the wheel.): The colour wheel governs which toner corrects which warmth, because complementary colours opposite each other cancel out. Violet sits opposite yellow, so a violet-based toner neutralizes the yellow left at the highest levels of lift. This is why most pale blonde toners lean violet or violet-ash.
  124. The correct answer is A (The tapering action can leave ends too thin and prone to breakage.): The straight razor is contraindicated on very fine or fragile hair, since the tapering action can leave the ends too thin and prone to breakage. Razors are actually used almost exclusively on wet hair, not restricted from it. Razor work produces a soft, tapered result, not a heavier blunt line.
  125. The correct answer is D (Plan a staged lightening approach with conservative volumes and a supportive base tone.): Fine, previously treated hair combined with a large five level lift target signals a real risk of damage, which argues for a staged approach using conservative developer strengths across more than one session rather than an aggressive single pass. At the same time, the scattered grey still needs a supportive base tone woven into the plan so coverage is not sacrificed while chasing the lift. Weighing hair condition, the size of the lift, and the grey coverage need together, rather than optimizing for only one of the three, is what a sound formulation plan requires here.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Correct the step now, since accuracy does not depend on what the client happens to notice.): A confirmed technical inconsistency such as a step from uneven elevation is a real defect regardless of whether the client, viewing the back of her own head indirectly, has noticed it herself. Professional standards call for correcting a known error rather than leaving it because the client has not identified it. A brief correction protects the finished quality of the work without requiring a separate future appointment.
  2. The correct answer is B (The developer strength, formula strength, and timing together set how much lift occurs.): Lightening powder is mixed with hydrogen peroxide developer, and the oxygen drives the reaction that diffuses and disperses melanin. The strength of the developer, the strength of the lightener formula, and the length of processing time together determine how many levels of lift the hair achieves. No single factor sets the final result on its own.
  3. The correct answer is A (The portion of strands caught between the shear's teeth on each pass.): Thinning shears remove only the portion of strands caught between their teeth on each pass, which is how they reduce bulk gradually rather than removing an entire section at once. Understanding this helps the stylist control how much density is removed with repeated passes. Handle length and other cosmetic details of the shears do not affect this specific cutting function.
  4. The correct answer is A (Do not proceed with that oxidative colour product on this client.): A visible reaction such as redness or swelling at the test site is a positive result indicating the client should not receive that oxidative colour product, and the stylist must not proceed with the planned service. Continuing despite a positive result risks a serious reaction during the actual application. The safe response is to stop and discuss alternative options with the client instead.
  5. The correct answer is B (The size of the clipper blade itself, without any guard.): The clipper blade itself has a size that determines how close it cuts against the skin without any attachment. Guard combs change the length only once one is attached to the blade. Comb angle and prior shear elevation do not determine the bare-blade cutting distance.
  6. The correct answer is C (The amount of contrast and the width of the transition zone define the ombre finish.): Ombre is the finished look of a strong, deliberate transition from a darker base near the root to a noticeably lighter end. It is defined by the contrast and the width of the transition zone, not by the tool that creates it. Balayage, foiling, or a combination can all build the same ombre finish.
  7. The correct answer is A (The two cuts still produce fundamentally different finished silhouettes.): Choosing stationary versus traveling is a consequence-heavy decision, since the two methods produce different silhouettes even with the same elevation and sectioning pattern. It is not true that guideline choice has no effect, or that the results would end up identical. A stationary guideline is also a fully valid choice for certain shapes, such as a one-length bob.
  8. The correct answer is C (Tone, also called reflect, describes a shade's warmth or coolness.): Tone, also known as reflect, is the dimension of colour that describes warmth or coolness, such as gold, ash, or violet. Level and tone are separate variables, so two shades can share a level while differing completely in tone. A stylist must track both to formulate an accurate result.
  9. The correct answer is B (Higher elevation and a traveling guideline build layering, which removes weight.): Higher elevation combined with a traveling guideline builds layering, which removes weight and creates a lighter, more mobile result compared to a one-length shape built at zero elevation. Wet versus dry cutting is a separate variable and does not by itself explain this lightness. Thinning shears can add texture, but elevation and guideline choice are what actually built this specific structural difference.
  10. The correct answer is B (Breakage cannot be reversed, so the hair can only be managed with protein treatments.): Breakage happens when the hair's protein structure is pushed past what it can tolerate, most often from overlapping lightener or leaving a chemical service on too long. It cannot be reversed once it occurs, so it can only be managed with protein treatments. Prevention means respecting processing times and refusing a service the hair cannot take.
  11. The correct answer is A (Around forty-five degrees, building moderate graduation and stack.): Elevation around forty-five degrees is commonly used to build moderate graduation, increasing stack through the back and sides without removing all the weight. Zero degrees instead keeps maximum weight at the perimeter with no graduation at all. Elevation carried far past one hundred eighty degrees removes far more weight than a moderate result requires.
  12. The correct answer is A (It is less targeted and puts more chemical stress on the hair.): Lightening powder works primarily on natural melanin, not on artificial dye, so it is less targeted for removing a tint. Using a stronger lightener also adds chemical stress that a purpose-built colour remover does not. The colour removal case should be handled with a reducer first, then reformulation onto the natural base.
  13. The correct answer is A (Held nearly flat against the scalp to produce a close, tight graduation.): Holding the comb nearly flat against the scalp minimizes the length left behind, producing the close, tight graduation the stylist wants at the nape. A wider comb angle would leave more length rather than a tight result. Comb angle is the direct control over how close the finished graduation sits.
  14. The correct answer is D (Ammonia is the alkalizing agent most commonly combined with peroxide.): Ammonia is the alkalizing agent traditionally paired with hydrogen peroxide in permanent colour, with MEA, monoethanolamine, used as a common alternative. The alkalizing agent swells the hair shaft and opens the cuticle so colour and developer can penetrate. Without this step, the formula could not lift or deposit effectively inside the cortex.
  15. The correct answer is D (Wet the hair and add slip from a cutting lotion.): Razor cutting is done almost exclusively on wet hair, because water and the slip from a conditioner or cutting lotion allow the blade to glide through the strand smoothly. On dry hair, a razor grabs and roughens the cuticle instead of cutting cleanly, producing a ragged result. Adjusting guard spacing or elevation does not correct for missing wet slip.
  16. The correct answer is B (Apply, work through evenly, then emulsify and rinse at the basin like a shampoo.): A gloss is worked through the hair and emulsified at the basin, which is why it behaves like a shampoo-in product rather than one sealed in sections. This even distribution is what lets it refresh tone without creating bands. Isolating it in foils would give an uneven, sectioned deposit instead.
  17. The correct answer is C (Cross-check the guideline repeatedly against the original reference points.): Checking the guideline against the original reference points, not only the previous section, prevents small inconsistencies from accumulating into a rounded rather than square outline. Comparing only to the immediately preceding section allows drift to build gradually without being caught. Frequent cross-checking against the original guide preserves the geometry of the shape.
  18. The correct answer is C (Colour buildup, corrected with a colour reducer or a gentle bleach bath on those sections.): Colour buildup happens when repeated permanent colour sits on the mid-lengths and ends while the root is not retouched to match. Those sections accumulate pigment and darken over time. Disciplined retouch application prevents it, and a colour reducer or bleach bath softens the built-up sections.
  19. The correct answer is D (A diagonal forward cutting line, angling down toward the face.): A diagonal forward cutting line angles down toward the face and is typically used to add length toward the front, framing the face with hair that grows progressively longer moving forward, which can soften a rounder face. A horizontal line instead keeps an even, level shape. A diagonal back or vertical line moves length in a different direction entirely.
  20. The correct answer is A (Keep the colour on new growth, blending the last half inch onto old colour lightly.): Full-strength formula sitting on already coloured hair deposits extra pigment and leaves a visible band. The retouch should stay on the new growth at the scalp, where the colour is genuinely needed. Blending just the boundary lightly avoids a hard line between old and new colour.
  21. The correct answer is B (Oiling the clipper regularly per the manufacturer's care instructions.): Clippers must be oiled per the manufacturer's instructions to prevent the motor from overheating and the blades from dragging through hair. Guard size, sectioning fineness, and guideline type do not address motor heat or blade drag. Regular oiling and cleaning of hair debris keep the mechanism running smoothly across a full day of services.
  22. The correct answer is D (The date the test was performed and its result should both be recorded.): Recording both the date the predisposition test was performed and its result on the client's record card creates a clear, professional record that protects the client and the salon. This documentation also supports continuity if a different stylist works with the client in the future. Skipping this step leaves no verifiable record that the required safety step was actually completed.
  23. The correct answer is A (Gently re-cut the longer sections down to match the shorter, correct ones.): Since hair can only be shortened and not lengthened, correcting uneven layers means gently re-cutting the sections that fall too long down to match the shorter, correctly cut sections. Trying to bring shorter sections up to match longer ones is not physically possible. Combing to natural fall first ensures the correction is measured accurately.
  24. The correct answer is C (Check the strand partway through to confirm coverage is developing evenly.): Checking a test strand partway through an extended processing time allows the stylist to confirm the grey coverage is developing evenly across both the resistant grey and the pigmented sections. This kind of monitoring helps catch an uneven result before the service is finished rather than after. Extended processing time on resistant hair calls for closer attention, not less.
  25. The correct answer is B (Taking the section thinner so less bulk is removed with each pass.): Because a razor removes more bulk per pass than a shear cut of similar length, taking thinner sections helps the stylist control how much is removed and avoid over-thinning. Thicker sections would increase the risk of excessive bulk removal in a single pass. Managing section size is part of controlling the overall razor cutting result.
  26. The correct answer is D (Yes, that resistant grey section may need a targeted approach for full coverage.): A localized area of grey, such as along the temples, can behave differently from the more heavily pigmented sections and may benefit from a targeted approach, such as extra saturation or a slightly adjusted base, to achieve full, even coverage. Treating the whole head identically without considering this localized difference risks an uneven finished result. Recognizing and adapting to a client's specific grey pattern is part of thorough formulation.
  27. The correct answer is C (Identify the shorter side and bring the longer side down to match it.): Once cross-checking confirms a genuine asymmetry, the correction is to identify the shorter side and bring the longer side down to match it, since hair cannot be added back once cut. Suggesting an extension technique goes beyond correcting a cutting error and changes the service entirely. Repeated cross-checking is specifically meant to confirm real discrepancies before any correction is made.
  28. The correct answer is A (To soften the line so the grow out does not show a hard demarcation.): Root smudging, also called root shadowing, blends a demi permanent shade close to the natural level into the first inch or two below the root, feathering the edge so there is no hard demarcation. It is commonly done at the same visit as a balayage or highlight service to make the grow out look intentional. It is also a useful corrective step when a previous colourist left a visible line.
  29. The correct answer is C (Shears to blend length and point cut the ends for a softer edge.): For longer beard styles, shears are used to blend length and remove bulk much as they would on a haircut, and point cutting the ends keeps the shape soft rather than leaving a hard, blunt edge. Clipper guards at the shortest setting would remove far more length than the client wants. A blunt trim with no blending would produce exactly the hard edge the client is trying to avoid.
  30. The correct answer is D (Selecting the lowest volume that can still meet the goal, given the hair's condition.): When hair is already compromised, hair condition becomes an especially important factor, and the stylist should lean toward the lowest developer volume that can still realistically achieve the requested goal. Using a stronger volume than necessary on already weakened hair increases the risk of further damage or breakage. Balancing the client's requested result against the hair's current condition is a key part of responsible formulation.
  31. The correct answer is B (A close, direct view of the transition zone itself under good lighting.): Checking the transition zone closely under good lighting, in addition to comparing overall symmetry between the two sides, helps the stylist catch subtle banding or unevenness in the blend that a wider view might miss. Symmetry alone does not guarantee the blend itself is smooth on each individual side. Both checks together give a more complete assessment of the finished fade.
  32. The correct answer is C (Underlying artificial tint is present, so a colour reducer is the correct tool.): Hair that refuses to lift despite a strong lightener is a strong signal of underlying artificial tint. Permanent artificial colour resists further lightening because its dye molecules sit differently in the cortex than natural melanin does. The correct tool is a colour reducer, not a stronger lightener, and recognizing tinted from natural hair is a core skill.
  33. The correct answer is A (A soft, tapered end rather than a blunt one.): The straight razor shaves through the strand at an angle rather than shearing it between two blades, which produces a soft, tapered end rather than a blunt one. This is different from the blunt, even line a shear produces at zero elevation. A razor-cut end can still be blended with surrounding layers.
  34. The correct answer is B (Removing the artificial colour so the natural level is visible again.): A client who dyed their hair too dark is a colour removal case first, and the stylist should use a colour reducer to break down the artificial pigment. Once the artificial colour is removed, the hair's natural level becomes visible again, and the stylist can formulate the intended colour onto that base. The same filler considerations apply if removal leaves the hair more porous.
  35. The correct answer is D (A cut checked on a tilted head can look balanced but appear uneven once level.): A cut that appears balanced while the client's head is tilted can look uneven the moment the head returns to a level, natural position, since the tilt distorts the visible reference points. Checking balance with the head held level ensures the assessment reflects how the cut will actually look day to day. This is why stylists specifically ask clients to hold their head level during a final check.
  36. The correct answer is A (Resistant grey needs full saturation at application to achieve even coverage.): Because resistant grey hair can be slower to accept colour, ensuring full saturation at application, especially in a commonly resistant area like the hairline, supports even, complete coverage once processing is finished. Partial saturation on resistant strands increases the risk of a patchy or incomplete result. Careful application technique matters as much as formula choice for grey coverage.
  37. The correct answer is D (Removing too much weight can loosen curl definition and cause the shape to lose form.): Layering curly hair should be approached with some caution, since removing too much underlying weight can loosen the curl's definition and cause the overall shape to lose its form. Enough weight is usually left in place to help the curl pattern hold together. Explaining this trade-off helps the client make an informed decision about how much layering to request.
  38. The correct answer is A (Accurately read the client's natural level in proper, neutral lighting.): Every formulation decision, including developer strength and product choice, depends on first establishing an accurate starting level under proper lighting. Reading the level incorrectly can lead to a formula that either underlifts or overlifts the hair. This is why level assessment is treated as the first step of any colour consultation.
  39. The correct answer is B (The crown, surrounding the apex on top of the head.): The crown is the area surrounding the apex where the hair growth pattern often whorls in a circular direction, commonly requiring extra attention because hair grows outward from that point. The nape, parietal ridge, and occipital bone describe other landmarks without this circular whorl pattern. A blended line through the crown can be harder to achieve for this reason.
  40. The correct answer is C (A yellow based toner should be applied, since yellow neutralizes violet tones.): Yellow sits opposite violet on the colour wheel, so a yellow based toner cancels the excess violet cast without introducing a new unwanted tone. This is a direct application of the complementary colour relationship taught in colour theory. Reaching for the wrong family, such as blue, would only intensify the cool, ashy problem.
  41. The correct answer is A (A dull or nicked blade increases the risk of skin injury.): The razor blade must be replaced or honed regularly to stay sharp, since a dull or nicked blade increases the risk of skin injury during use. A dull blade does not improve taper control, it makes the cut less predictable. Straight razors, like clippers and shears, all require regular maintenance for safety.
  42. The correct answer is C (Apply a blue based toner, since blue sits opposite orange on the wheel.): Blue sits directly opposite orange on the colour wheel, so a blue based toner neutralizes orange warmth left over from lightening. Choosing a toner based on wheel position rather than colour name produces a predictable, balanced result. Selecting the wrong complementary shade can shift the hair toward a new unwanted tone instead of correcting it.
  43. The correct answer is A (Every area receives consistent, systematic attention while cutting.): Sectioning lets the stylist work through the head systematically so every area receives consistent attention, rather than being cut freehand and unevenly. It does not produce a razor taper on its own, nor does it remove the need to control elevation. Sectioning also has no effect on how hair shrinks as it dries.
  44. The correct answer is D (The level and tone of the finished result can shift away from the swatch.): Diluting a formula beyond its specified ratio changes how much oxidant is available for lifting and building colour, which can shift both the level and the tone away from what the swatch predicted. This is not simply a weaker version of the same result, it is a different chemical balance. Following the manufacturer's specified ratio protects the predictability of the finished colour.
  45. The correct answer is B (A vertical parting pattern taken up and down the head.): A vertical parting pattern, taken up and down the head, is common in graduated and layered cuts because it lets the stylist follow the curve of the head consistently. A horizontal pattern instead suits a one-length shape with a level perimeter. A pattern confined to only the front hairline would not organize the whole graduated shape.
  46. The correct answer is B (Permanent oxidative colour supports both lifting and a full tone change together.): Because permanent, oxidative colour both lifts the natural pigment and deposits new pigment, it is the category capable of delivering a noticeably lighter result together with a full change in tone in one formula. Categories limited to depositing pigment alone cannot achieve the lifting half of this request. Selecting permanent colour matches the product's real capability to the client's combined goal.
  47. The correct answer is D (Check the direction of natural fall around the cowlick before cutting.): Graduated shapes are less forgiving of cowlicks and irregular growth patterns at the nape, so checking how the hair naturally falls in that area helps the stylist adjust elevation and direction accordingly. Ignoring it risks an uneven stack once the hair settles. Assessing natural fall before committing to elevation is standard practice.
  48. The correct answer is C (The formula should include a strong green base to neutralize the red tone.): Green sits directly opposite red on the colour wheel, so a green based corrective formula is the appropriate choice to cancel the unwanted red pigment in the mid-lengths. Matching the corrector to the true complementary colour prevents overcorrecting into a different unwanted cast. This decision reflects core neutralization theory rather than guesswork.
  49. The correct answer is D (An increased likelihood of an accidental slip from fatigue.): A stylist should maintain a stable, balanced stance and proper tool grip throughout a cutting service, since fatigue and poor posture increase the likelihood of an accidental slip. Poor posture does not improve cutting precision, and it is not simply a comfort issue when sharp tools are involved. Posture also has no direct effect on how quickly a blade dulls.
  50. The correct answer is C (Red and blue combine to create the secondary colour violet.): Violet is created by combining the primary colours red and blue in equal parts. It sits opposite yellow on the wheel, which is why yellow based correctors are used against unwanted violet or ash overcast. Knowing which primaries build each secondary colour helps a stylist reason through a formula rather than memorizing brand names.
  51. The correct answer is A (Elevating interior sections enough to remove bulk without shortening the perimeter.): Elevating the interior sections enough to remove bulk, while keeping the perimeter at a lower elevation closer to the requested length, allows the cut to reduce density without sacrificing the overall length the client wants. Zero elevation throughout would not address the bulk concern at all. Adjusting interior elevation is the standard way to manage density in thick hair while preserving length.
  52. The correct answer is A (It tells them how light the shade is, but nothing about its warmth or coolness.): The level number communicates only how light or dark a shade is on the ten point scale, not whether it will read warm or cool. Tone is a separate value that must be read alongside the level to understand the full shade. This is why professional charts always pair a level number with a tone indicator.
  53. The correct answer is B (A fuzzy, frizzy appearance that cannot be fixed until regrowth.): Overusing thinning shears, or texturizing too close to the scalp, creates a fuzzy, frizzy appearance that cannot be corrected until the hair grows out. It does not create a heavier perimeter line or correct graduation problems. A conservative, test-as-you-go approach is the safer way to avoid this outcome.
  54. The correct answer is D (Recommend a maintenance toning schedule and a purple or blue shampoo.): Brassiness that develops weeks after a successful service is a fading and maintenance issue rather than a formulation error. Toners fade faster than the underlying lift, so the warm undertone gradually reasserts itself. A purple or blue-toned shampoo and a maintenance toning schedule directly address that cause.
  55. The correct answer is C (It requires more careful handling and professional sharpening.): A convex edge is hollow ground and razor sharp, giving an extremely clean cut with minimal pull, but it requires more careful handling and professional sharpening than a standard beveled edge. It is not restricted to razor work and still needs normal pivot maintenance. It is fully capable of producing a clean line on suitable hair.
  56. The correct answer is D (Strand test the grey area and adjust saturation or timing for that section.): Uneven results often come from a formula applied without accounting for different starting levels in different areas of the same head, such as grey resistant hair at the temples against darker hair elsewhere. A strand test before the full service catches most of these before they become a corrective visit. The stylist should adjust the approach for the resistant section rather than treating the whole head identically.
  57. The correct answer is B (Eliminating any visible line of demarcation between each guard length.): The defining skill in fade and taper work is eliminating visible lines between guard lengths so the transition reads as one smooth blend rather than a series of stacked bands. This is achieved with flicking, over-comb style motions worked through each transition zone. Without this blending, the guard changes remain visible as distinct steps.
  58. The correct answer is B (The regrowth is natural level hair that needs a matching, level-lifting formula.): The regrowth area is untouched, natural level hair, and matching it accurately to previously lightened lengths often requires the lifting and depositing capability that only a permanent oxidative formula provides. A demi-permanent gloss deposits tone but cannot lift, so it would not resolve a level difference between regrowth and the rest of the hair. Choosing the formula based on what the regrowth actually needs is standard professional practice.
  59. The correct answer is C (Test the guard on a small, low-risk section first.): Because the exact length a guard produces can vary slightly between brands, a competent stylist checks the actual length on the specific clipper system in use and tests on a small, low-risk section before committing to a full head. Assuming every brand matches exactly, or trusting the printed number alone, risks an unwanted result. Skipping the test entirely removes any chance to catch a mismatch early.
  60. The correct answer is C (A colour remover, which breaks down the artificial dye so the natural level shows.): A client whose colour turned out too dark is a colour removal case first, because the goal is to remove artificial dye, not to lift natural melanin. A colour remover breaks down the synthetic dye and lets the natural level show again. The stylist can then formulate the intended colour onto that base, adding a filler if removal left the hair porous.
  61. The correct answer is A (Ninety degrees, held consistently from section to section throughout.): Uniform layering elevates each section to the same angle, most often ninety degrees, and cuts it to match the previous section's length. This consistency spreads volume evenly through the interior of the cut rather than stacking weight at any one point. It is the technique behind many classic layered bobs and mid-length shapes.
  62. The correct answer is D (The chosen formula may not lift enough to reach the client's desired target.): Misjudging the starting level by even one shade can lead to a formula built for the wrong starting point, which often means the finished colour does not lift as far as the client expected. Accurate level reading is the foundation every later decision depends on. A misread level is a common, preventable cause of a disappointing result.
  63. The correct answer is D (Which guide method will most reliably keep the outline consistent for this cut.): The choice between a stationary and a travelling guide should be based on which method will most reliably produce a consistent perimeter outline for the specific shape being cut. Both methods are valid tools, and the appropriate one depends on the design and the stylist's control over that particular technique. Factors unrelated to the cut itself should not drive this technical decision.
  64. The correct answer is D (10 volume developer is generally chosen for depositing with little to no lift.): 10 volume developer produces little to no lift on the natural level, making it the choice when a stylist mainly wants to deposit colour or refresh tone. It is also commonly paired with grey coverage formulas close to the client's natural shade. Higher volumes are reserved for services that require noticeable lightening.
  65. The correct answer is B (To catch any asymmetry between the two sides before finishing the service.): Facial hair asymmetry is highly visible at conversational distance, so comparing both sides against the centre of the chin throughout the service helps catch any unevenness before the client leaves the chair. This kind of repeated visual check is standard practice in beard trimming. Waiting until the very end to check symmetry risks missing an error that is harder to correct later.
  66. The correct answer is A (Scalp heat speeds processing, so the mid-lengths and ends get the longer developing time.): The scalp emits body heat that speeds the colour development at the root. Applying colour to the more slowly processing mid-lengths and ends first, then to the scalp, lets the entire strand reach the same level. If the scalp were done first it would overdevelop and come out darker than the rest.
  67. The correct answer is C (A low guard number, since lower numbers leave hair shorter.): A low guard number leaves the hair shorter and closer to the scalp, which fits the goal of a very close length while still leaving a thin layer of hair rather than bare skin. A high guard number would leave hair noticeably longer instead. Using the bare blade would cut directly against the skin with no guard length at all, which is not what was requested.
  68. The correct answer is B (Each foil packet is independent, so one can be removed while the other keeps processing.): Because each foil packet is independent of the others, a stronger formula near the crown can be removed when it reaches its target while the gentler foils continue to process. This timing control is the basis of multi-tonal work in one sitting. Running a single timing across the whole head would force one area to over- or under-develop.
  69. The correct answer is D (Clipper-over-comb technique used to build a smooth, blended gradient.): Clipper-over-comb is a core skill for creating a smooth gradient between shorter and longer areas rather than one uniform length, since the comb angle controls exactly how much length is left at each point. A single guard applied uniformly, or the bare blade throughout, would produce one flat length instead of a gradient. A stationary guideline is a shear concept and does not apply to clipper tapering.
  70. The correct answer is A (Test and assess, then remove unwanted colour, fill, then tone and deposit.): A corrective service is sequenced to assess and test first, remove what needs removing with a colour reducer or a bleach bath, correct the base with a filler and further lightening only if the hair supports it, then tone and deposit the final shade. Skipping ahead, such as toning before the underlying warmth is neutralised, turns a correctable problem into a service that has to be redone. Testing at each stage keeps the service controlled.
  71. The correct answer is B (Use moderate, curl-by-curl layering that removes some weight without over-thinning.): Because heavy layering can loosen curl definition and reduce apparent length on tightly coiled hair, a moderate, curl-by-curl approach lets the stylist add movement while preserving enough weight to keep the curl pattern intact and the length looking full. Cutting the hair stretched straight would risk the opposite problem, an unexpectedly short result once the curl reforms. Balancing both client goals requires judgment about how much weight the curl pattern can lose before it loses definition.
  72. The correct answer is B (A semi-permanent colour fits this request, since it deposits without lifting.): Semi-permanent colour deposits pigment without a developer reaction and cannot lift the natural level, which matches a client who wants only to refresh tone gently. It fades gradually over a number of washes rather than growing out at the root. This makes it a lower commitment option compared with a permanent oxidative service.
  73. The correct answer is B (Reviewing the cut once it is fully dry and styled the way the client wears it.): A dry, styled check confirms how the cut will actually look day to day, since curl pattern and shrinkage are already reflected once the hair is dry and styled. Wetting curly hair back down would reintroduce the elongation the dry cut was specifically designed to account for. This final check applies to curly cutting the same way it applies to any finished haircut.
  74. The correct answer is A (The target colour had no consistent surface to grab onto along the strand.): Skipping the filler and going straight to the target shade is a common cause of a corrective service turning patchy or fading unevenly. The target colour has no consistent base to grab onto, so it takes strongly in some places and poorly in others. Filling first, then laying the shade over an evened-out base, is the standard sequence.
  75. The correct answer is C (Just above the natural break where the neck hair growth stops.): The neckline is typically cut just above the natural break where growth stops, which keeps the beard looking intentional rather than blending unchecked into the neck. Cutting well below that point would remove hair the client may want to keep as part of the beard shape. Following the natural growth break is the standard reference point.
  76. The correct answer is B (Sections were grabbed too large or painted too fast for controlled saturation.): Because freehand painting relies on the stylist's hand rather than a foil boundary, consistent brush pressure and disciplined section size matter a great deal. Grabbing sections too large or painting too fast gives uneven saturation along the strands. That inconsistency is a frequent cause of patchy lift in balayage.
  77. The correct answer is A (Work in overlapping passes with steady comb angle and constant checking.): Overlapping passes, combined with a steady comb angle and frequent checking of the section from different angles, are what produce a smooth graduated blend rather than a choppy result. A single uninterrupted pass makes it harder to control consistency across the whole area. Constant checking during the process catches unevenness before it becomes a larger error.
  78. The correct answer is D (Keep each sectioning divide a consistent size and saturate each foil evenly.): Uneven results from a rushed or uneven sectioning pattern are one of the most common preventable causes of patchy colour. Clean, even partings let the stylist control saturation and timing consistently across the head. Correcting the section size and the saturation per foil fixes the cause rather than working around it with a stronger developer or longer timing.
  79. The correct answer is D (An inconsistent thumb-and-ring-finger grip changing the blade angle.): An inconsistent grip changes the blade angle from stroke to stroke, which both fatigues the hand and produces an uneven cutting line over a long service. Correcting hand position restores a steady, repeatable angle. Hair texture, guideline choice, and wet versus dry cutting do not typically explain this specific fatigue and drift pattern.
  80. The correct answer is C (Use a blue based toner to neutralise the orange and plan fillers if needed.): Orange hair that already reads orange after lightening is a blue toner case, because blue is complementary to orange. Because the hair is fragile, the stylist should avoid pushing it with more lightener and instead neutralise the warmth, then restore lost structure with a filler if the target shade needs a uniform base. Violet is not complementary to orange and would leave a muddy tone.
  81. The correct answer is B (Cutting at least partially dry, checking length at several points during drying rather than finishing wet.): Because coily hair can shrink dramatically as the curl pattern contracts, cutting at least partially dry and checking length at multiple points during drying is the reliable way to confirm the final result before removing more length than intended. Trusting a fully wet measurement risks a finished cut far shorter than expected once the curl springs back. Thinning shears address bulk, not shrinkage, and avoiding any further check during drying removes the one safeguard this texture specifically needs.
  82. The correct answer is C (Recommend multiple sessions with conservative developer and a cool toning plan.): Balancing a large lift distance, a strong preference against warmth, and a sensitive scalp calls for a conservative, multi-session strategy using the lowest developer volume that will realistically progress the lift, followed by a deliberately cool toning plan once the underlying warmth is exposed. Attempting the full five level change in one pass with a strong developer would raise both damage and scalp risk unnecessarily. Weighing all three factors together, rather than optimizing for speed alone, protects both the hair and the client's stated preferences.
  83. The correct answer is C (Length increases gradually from a short nape up toward the crown.): A graduated bob is cut with the nape shortest and the length increasing as the sections move up toward the crown. This produces the characteristic stacked, weighted look at the back of the shape. The front is often left longer for a face-framing angle while the graduation principle stays the same.
  84. The correct answer is A (The red stage is a predictable point in the underlying pigment progression.): The law of colour explains that hair passes through a predictable sequence of warm stages, often beginning near red, as the natural melanin is broken down during lifting. Seeing this stage mid-process is expected rather than a sign of a problem. Recognizing it as normal helps a new stylist stay confident and plan the toning step that follows.
  85. The correct answer is D (Layering, typically built at higher elevation at or beyond ninety degrees.): Layering removes the connected weight line entirely and creates hair of varying lengths that move independently, typically built at higher elevation at or beyond ninety degrees. Graduation instead keeps a defined, weighted perimeter line rather than removing it. A stationary guideline and zero-degree elevation are both associated with a connected, blunt weight line, the opposite effect.
  86. The correct answer is D (The salts can react with peroxide, generating heat and causing breakage.): Metallic dyes deposit metallic salts onto the hair shaft, which can react with the hydrogen peroxide in permanent colour or lightener. That reaction can generate significant heat at the scalp, discolour unpredictably, or cause severe breakage. Asking about metallic dye history and testing protects the client from a service that cannot be corrected on the spot.
  87. The correct answer is B (Proper cleaning and disinfection performed between every single client.): Clippers must be cleaned of hair debris and properly disinfected between clients as part of standard infection control practice. Reusing the same guard, rereading consultation notes, or switching tools does not address the infection control requirement. Skipping disinfection between clients is a genuine safety lapse regardless of how routine the cut is.
  88. The correct answer is C (Level 1 represents the darkest natural shade, a true black.): The international level system runs from 1 to 10, with level 1 assigned to the darkest natural colour, true black. Level 10 sits at the opposite end as the lightest, palest blonde. Using consistent numbers rather than brand names lets stylists compare shades across different manufacturers.
  89. The correct answer is C (Both techniques risk thinning fragile ends, so a conservative amount of either should be used.): Both razor tapering and heavy thinning shear work can leave fine, fragile ends too thin and prone to breakage, so the safer path is a conservative, test-as-you-go amount of whichever technique is chosen rather than assuming one tool is automatically safe. Declaring either tool universally safe or universally forbidden ignores how each actually behaves on fragile hair. A blanket refusal to use either technique is also not supported, since controlled, light application of either can still be appropriate.
  90. The correct answer is D (Including enough natural or natural-warm base tone to achieve full deposit.): With a significant percentage of grey, the formula needs enough natural or natural-warm base tone to supply full deposit on hair that has no underlying pigment of its own to blend with. Skipping this base risks an uneven, overly cool, or insufficiently covered result. Estimating the grey percentage during consultation guides how much base tone the formula should include.
  91. The correct answer is A (A skin fade starts at bare skin while a taper stops short of bare skin.): A skin fade blends from bare skin at its lowest point up through a sequence of guards into longer hair above, while a taper shortens gradually at the perimeter without ever going down to skin. Both use the same graduated guard principle but differ in their starting point. This distinction defines the two techniques.
  92. The correct answer is A (A fine tight weave lifts a small amount of hair and gives a soft blended look.): A fine, tight weave lifts a small percentage of the hair and gives a subtle, blended result, while a thick, chunky weave lifts more hair and gives bold streaks. Weave density directly controls how much hair is lifted with each foil. The stylist should match the weave to the look the client wants.
  93. The correct answer is B (A shallow angle drawn through the strand smoothly.): The blade should be drawn through the strand at a shallow angle so it shaves rather than tears the hair, with the strand held under firm, even tension. A perpendicular or overly steep angle risks tearing rather than shaving the hair cleanly. Elevation is an unrelated variable used for shear cutting, not razor angle.
  94. The correct answer is A (Previously lightened hair can break, since it has no melanin left and no structural reserve.): Hair that was lightened in a previous session has already lost its melanin, so there is no pigment left to protect it from further chemical action. Following with more lightener also draws on structural reserves the cortex has already given up. This overlap is a leading cause of breakage in lightening services.
  95. The correct answer is D (Level the client's head and re-check the comb angle against the scalp.): A tilted head changes the apparent angle of every section and produces a line that looks crooked once the client sits upright again. Levelling the head restores an accurate reference for the comb and shears. This is essential in one-length work because there is no layering to hide an error.
  96. The correct answer is D (Hair long and smooth enough to pull cleanly through the cap holes.): A highlighting cap works only on hair long enough and smooth enough to pull cleanly through the cap, and it gives a fine, uniform highlight because the cap limits how much hair is pulled at each hole. It cannot easily produce the bold placement or multi formula work that foiling allows. It is also uncomfortable on curly or very short hair.
  97. The correct answer is C (More interior length is cut shorter relative to the perimeter, building graduation.): As elevation increases from zero toward ninety degrees, more of the interior length is cut shorter relative to the perimeter, which builds graduation and begins to remove weight. The perimeter becomes lighter, not heavier, as elevation rises. Elevation changes the shape of the cut meaningfully, and it does not on its own shorten the overall length being held and cut.
  98. The correct answer is C (A bleach bath, diluted and worked through, which softens the hair's uneven tone gently.): A bleach bath is a gentler even-out tool because the shampoo base dilutes the active lightening agents for a softer, slower lift. It removes buildup of old artificial colour or lightens by a small, controlled amount. This suits hair whose condition would not tolerate a full-strength lightener.
  99. The correct answer is A (Layers or angles that reduce the jawline's visual strength.): A square face is often softened with layers or angles that reduce the visual strength of the jawline rather than emphasizing it further. A completely blunt line following the jaw, or added width at the jaw, would tend to emphasize the angularity instead of softening it. A plan that ignores the jawline entirely would not address the client's specific request.
  100. The correct answer is B (Temporary colour sits in the cuticle and rinses out in one shampoo.): Temporary colour coats only the outside of the hair shaft in the cuticle layer and is designed to be removed with a single shampoo. It cannot lighten the natural level because it never penetrates the cortex. This makes it the gentlest and least durable category of colour product.
  101. The correct answer is A (Send the shears for professional sharpening before continuing.): Pulling and crimping instead of a clean shear through the hair is the classic sign of a dull blade, not a tension problem. Professional sharpening restores the cutting edge so the shear parts hair cleanly again. Adjusting tension, switching tools, or changing angle will not fix a genuinely dull blade.
  102. The correct answer is C (A bleach bath worked through the built up sections to soften them gradually.): A bleach bath is a diluted, gentler version of standard lightening, used to remove buildup of old artificial colour gently or lighten hair by a small controlled amount when full strength lightener would be too aggressive. Because the shampoo base is worked through like a shampoo, it gives a softer, slower lift. It suits hair that cannot tolerate a harsher chemical service.
  103. The correct answer is C (Wider-spaced teeth, which remove more hair per stroke.): Wider-spaced teeth on a thinning shear remove more hair per stroke, making them suited to heavier, coarser bulk removal on a dense section. Finer, closely spaced teeth remove less per stroke and are meant for delicate blending instead. A straight serrated edge or a convex edge does not offer the same graduated bulk removal.
  104. The correct answer is D (The test must still be performed, even if it delays or reschedules the service.): Time pressure does not justify skipping a required predisposition test, especially for a first time client, since the purpose of the test is to prevent a genuine safety risk. The correct decision is to perform the test as required, even if that means rescheduling the actual colour service. Prioritizing schedule convenience over a mandatory safety step is not acceptable professional practice.
  105. The correct answer is D (At the crown or front hairline, then blend shorter sections up to it.): Long layered cuts typically start with a guideline at the crown or front hairline, then blend progressively longer sections into it toward the perimeter. This keeps the overall length intact while adding interior movement. Starting elsewhere would make it difficult to control how the layers blend into the length.
  106. The correct answer is B (Explain that tone must also be selected deliberately to finish the formula.): A mentor should point out that level and tone are two separate decisions made together, and reaching the correct level says nothing about whether the finished tone will read as warm, cool, or neutral. Treating level as the whole formula skips a decision that directly affects how the client's final colour looks. Both variables need deliberate attention for a complete, accurate result.
  107. The correct answer is B (A traveling guideline, also called a mobile guideline.): A traveling guideline, also called a mobile guideline, is carried forward from one subsection into the next by picking up a small piece of the previously cut section as the reference point. A stationary guideline instead stays fixed and does not move forward this way. A horizontal cutting line and zero-degree elevation describe separate cutting variables, not the guideline itself.
  108. The correct answer is A (Toner applied over under lifted hair gives a temporary fix that fades quickly.): If the hair did not lift enough, toner applied over insufficiently lifted hair produces only a temporary, quickly fading fix, because toner deposits colour rather than lifting. The stylist must re evaluate whether further lightening is safe for the hair's current condition. Reformulating the toner alone will not solve the underlying lift problem.
  109. The correct answer is B (Horizontal partings, combed flat and compared against neighbouring sections.): A cut built on vertical sections is cross-checked with horizontal partings, since re-parting in a different direction reveals unevenness the original sectioning could not show. Using the same vertical partings again would not expose the same kind of hidden inconsistency. This directional change is the whole purpose of the cross-checking step.
  110. The correct answer is B (Consider whether a lower volume developer might achieve the same result safely.): If a client's hair lifts faster than typical at a given developer strength, it is worth considering whether a lower volume could achieve the same result with less risk to hair condition on future visits. This kind of strand test observation helps the stylist tailor formulation to the individual client over time. Automatically increasing strength would move in the wrong direction from what the result actually showed.
  111. The correct answer is D (Apply light point cutting carefully, avoiding removal of too much curl-supporting weight.): Light, careful point cutting can add texture to curly ends while avoiding the removal of too much weight, which helps preserve the curl definition the client wants to keep. Heavy point cutting risks the same weight-loss problem as heavy layering on curly hair. Point cutting is not inherently off-limits for curly hair, but it should be applied with awareness of how curl pattern responds to weight removal.
  112. The correct answer is A (Mixed porosity along the strand or uneven sectioning and saturation.): Uneven colour results usually trace back to inconsistent sectioning or saturation, hair with mixed porosity along its own length, or a formula applied without accounting for different starting levels in different areas of the same head. These are often caught by a thorough consultation and a strand test before the full service. Reading the hair's varying condition is part of the diagnosis.
  113. The correct answer is B (It reveals unevenness the original partings could not show on their own.): Cross-checking re-parts the hair in a direction different from the original cutting sections, revealing any strand that falls longer or shorter than its neighbours that the original partings could not show. A cut on vertical sections is typically cross-checked with horizontal partings and vice versa. This step is a standard part of finishing any haircut before the client leaves.
  114. The correct answer is A (Apply a colour filler to redeposit missing pigment and even out porosity.): A colour filler corrects porous, damaged hair before the final colour goes on. It redeposits missing pigment and evens out porosity so the hair accepts the next shade uniformly. Skipping the filler leaves the target colour grabbing at inconsistent points along the strand.
  115. The correct answer is C (The client's general face shape category during consultation.): Basic face shape analysis compares the width and length of the forehead, cheekbones, and jawline to identify common categories such as oval, round, square, heart, oblong, and diamond. It does not by itself determine elevation, drying shrinkage, or a clipper guard number. Face shape informs how cutting line and elevation choices are later applied during planning.
  116. The correct answer is C (A little lightening powder, developer, and shampoo, diluted to give a softer lift.): A bleach bath mixes a small amount of lightening powder with developer and shampoo, which dilutes the active lightening agents and gives a softer, slower lift. Because the shampoo base is worked through like a shampoo rather than painted on thick, it is gentler than full strength lightening. It is the right choice when the client's hair condition will not tolerate a stronger formula.
  117. The correct answer is B (It is stronger and more aerated, so it is more likely to irritate the scalp.): An off scalp formula is a stronger, more highly aerated powder built for foiled highlights or balayage on the mid lengths and ends, where it will not sit against the skin. It is more likely to cause scalp irritation and should not be used for a full head application starting at the scalp. On scalp formulas are gentler and buffered for direct skin contact.
  118. The correct answer is B (Changing one variable without the others produces an unpredictable final result.): Ratio, developer volume, and processing time work together as a connected system, so adjusting one without considering the others can produce a result that does not match the manufacturer's published outcome. Training a new stylist to treat these as interdependent, rather than separate, variables supports predictable formulation. This is a foundational principle behind reliable colour work.
  119. The correct answer is C (Paraphenylenediamine, commonly abbreviated PPD, is a well known allergen in colour products.): Paraphenylenediamine, abbreviated PPD, is a recognized allergen found in many oxidative colour formulas and a leading reason the trade requires predisposition testing. A sensitized client can experience a serious reaction if exposed without warning. This is why the test is mandatory rather than optional before a first time or repeat colour service.
  120. The correct answer is A (Verify the specific ratio required for that particular product line and category.): Because mixing ratios can vary by manufacturer and by product category, a stylist switching between product lines or categories should verify the specific ratio required rather than assuming the same ratio carries over automatically. Demi-permanent and permanent lines do not always share identical formulation guidelines. Checking the manufacturer's chart before mixing avoids an inconsistent or unpredictable result.
  121. The correct answer is D (Select a filler and a target shade that bring the level down and control the tone.): Correcting a too bright result means bringing the level down and controlling the tone, which typically means using a filler to even the porous surface and then a target shade that deposits the deeper, cooler colour. Applying more lightener only lifts further and makes the problem worse. Reading the current level and the underlying warmth decides the formula.
  122. The correct answer is A (Choose a level 7 shade with an ash or cool based tone rather than gold.): A client who dislikes brassy or golden warmth is best served by selecting a level 7 shade coded with an ash or cool reflect, since tone is the variable that controls warmth independent of lightness. Simply changing the level would not address the client's actual concern about brassiness. Matching the stated preference requires adjusting tone, not level.
  123. The correct answer is C (Grabbing sections too large or painting too fast across the hair.): Because freehand painting relies on the stylist's hand rather than a foil boundary, consistent brush pressure and disciplined section size matter even more than in foiling. Grabbing sections too large or painting too fast is a frequent cause of uneven lift in freehand work. Sectioning discipline is also a common root cause of patchy results in general.
  124. The correct answer is D (Toner deposits colour, so it will only mask the warmth until it fades.): A toner is a demi-permanent or semi-permanent colour that deposits tone, but it does not lift. No toner can make genuinely under-lightened orange hair become a true ash blonde. If the hair did not lift enough, the real fix is to re-evaluate further lightening for its condition, not to tone over it.
  125. The correct answer is D (Water, peroxide, and ammonia, which make metallic salts discolour or bubble.): A metallic salt test uses a small strand in a solution of water and peroxide with a few drops of ammonia. A strand containing metallic salts will discolour, heat up, or bubble within a short time. This precaution matters because metallic salts react with the peroxide in permanent colour and lightener, generating heat and risking breakage.
